# Management-Governance Module — UBER (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Uber's stewardship read is capped by one unresolved critical red flag: a federal derivative lawsuit filed mid-2026 names CEO Dara Khosrowshahi, Chairman Ronald Sugar, and every sitting director, alleging the board's own proxy statements on safety oversight were false — forcing this module's verdict no better than serious governance concerns. Beneath that cap, the operating record is real: four straight years of delivered EBIT and free-cash-flow improvement, and mostly-kept promises. Capital allocation is separately capped by a serial-acquirer pattern — a written-off $1.1bn Drizly deal, a reversed Careem stake, and a pending $14.8bn debt-funded Delivery Hero bid. Insider ownership is thin (0.18%), and incentive alignment cannot be assessed — the governing proxy statement was never filed into this data pool.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict: Serious governance concerns** — this category is a rule-driven cap, not a finding of proven wrongdoing. It is forced by MODULE_RULES.md's hard rule that any Critical red flag firing in this module caps the verdict "no better than 'Serious governance concerns'" regardless of the composite score. Two Critical-materiality flags fired this run: RF-MGT-005 (an unresolved shareholder derivative lawsuit naming the sitting CEO, Chairman, and entire board) and RF-CAP-004 (the serial-acquirer pattern culminating in the $14.8bn debt-funded Delivery Hero bid). Absent those two triggers, the underlying board mechanics (90% independent, split chair, single-class stock, no promoter, contractual pledging ban) and a genuinely delivered four-year operating turnaround would support a "Standard / mixed" read — but the rule does not permit averaging a Critical flag away, and this synthesis does not soften it.
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** N — `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` tested all 8 disqualifiers (audit qualification, pledging >50%, RPT >25%, auditor churn, restatement, enforcement, customer concentration, negative OCF) and found none triggered (0 of 5 near-misses in band). The verdict cap above comes from this module's own Critical-red-flag rule, not from the disqualifier scan.
- Management quality /100: **60** *(capped from a raw 71 by RF-MGT-005 — unresolved adverse integrity signal, §24 Filter 1)*
- Capital allocation /100: **50** *(capped from a raw 61 by RF-CAP-004 — serial-acquirer pattern, §24 Filter 4)*
- Incentive alignment /100: **5** *(Insufficient Data — hard cap is max 50, non-binding; the constraint is the missing proxy, not the cap ceiling)*
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **60** *(capped from a blended ~64 — Board & Shareholder Rights 69, Ownership Alignment 59 — by the missing-ownership-data cap)*
- Disclosure candor /100: **53** *(RF-MGT-005's candor cap of max 60 is non-binding here since the raw score already sits below it)*
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse)*: **70** *(floor of 60 from RF-CAP-004 is non-binding; set above the floor to reflect two Critical- and four High/Medium-materiality flags stacking — RF-MGT-005, RF-CAP-004, RF-DISC-001, RF-DISC-002, RF-CAP-001, RF-REG-002)*
- Data quality /100: **65** *(from 00 — most governance inputs present and usable; the DEF 14A proxy, the single highest-value document, is genuinely absent and blocks two entire specialists)*
- Overall usefulness /100: **68** *(capped at max 70 by the missing-proxy rule)*
- Insider ownership (one line): ~0.18% of shares outstanding held by officers/directors; CEO holds 0.060% ($87.8mm); every "purchase" in the 6-month pool window is a $0-value RSU vest or an ambiguous onboarding-linked transaction — no genuine open-market cash purchase found; modest net selling in both 3- and 6-month windows [04].
- Biggest governance signal (one line): an unresolved federal shareholder derivative lawsuit (filed N.D. Cal., reported Jun/Jul-2026) names the sitting CEO, Chairman, and every director, alleging the board ignored an internal safety-risk model for five years (2017–2022) and that its own 2024–2026 proxy statements about safety oversight were false — unproven, but it names the exact people this report judges and, per this module's own rules, caps the entire verdict [01, 05, 06 — RF-MGT-005].
- **Governance Score /100** = 0.20×50 (CapAlloc) + 0.18×5 (Incentive) + 0.18×60 (ShFriendliness) + 0.16×53 (Candor) + 0.16×60 (MgmtQuality) + 0.12×(100−70) (GovRisk) = 10.00 + 0.90 + 10.80 + 8.48 + 9.60 + 3.60 = **43.38 ≈ 43**
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100** = 43 × 60/100 = **26**
- **Governance Rating: Weak** (40–54 band on the raw formula; independently forced no-better-than-Weak by the Critical-red-flag rule — both paths agree)
- **Confidence Score /100** (source quality): **60** — audited-filing data (10-K, 10-Qs, board composition, RPT balances) is Tier-1/high-confidence (4–5), but the single most decision-relevant fact this run — the derivative-lawsuit allegations — rests entirely on two unverified, dated web articles (confidence 2/5), and the compensation/ownership-percentage reads rest on a data-vendor substitute for the absent proxy (confidence 3–4)
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** **6 / 2**

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** The unresolved derivative lawsuit (RF-MGT-005) lands at the same time as a serial-acquirer pattern that already produced one full write-off (Drizly, $1.1bn, shut within 2.5 years) and one reversed divestiture (Careem, sold to minority in 2023, bought back to control by mid/late-2026) — and management's response is to commit $14.8bn, funded mostly by new debt, to the largest deal in company history (Delivery Hero) [02, 01].
- **Strongest bull point (steelman):** The CEO delivered a real, checkable operating turnaround — four straight years of EBIT and free-cash-flow improvement, four straight quarters of Adjusted EBITDA guidance beaten (above the high end the last two), a 90%-independent board with a split chair/CEO and fully independent committees, no promoter, no dual-class stock, and a contractual pledging ban that reconciles cleanly with the disqualifier scan [01, 05].
- **Single killer risk:** If the derivative lawsuit survives a motion to dismiss and discovery produces evidence that the board's own proxy statements about safety oversight were knowingly false, this converts from an unproven allegation into a proven integrity finding implicating the entire sitting board — which would very likely escalate to a hard disqualifier at the next module refresh [01, 05, 06].
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** The CFO-transition statement was specific and disclaimed any accounting cause; pledging is contractually banned company-wide and confirmed clean by the disqualifier scan; the §24 Filter 6 unaligned-owner test does not trip (no controlling holder exists); and buybacks have genuinely reduced net share count in the most recent 15 months for the first time since the 2019 IPO [01, 04, 02].

## 3. Reconciliation

Two disagreements surface across the specialists, both resolved conservatively:

1. **Strong operating execution (01's raw 71/100) vs. a management-quality score the synthesis reports at 60/100.** Agent 01 documents a genuinely strong, checkable promise-vs-delivery record — four straight years of delivered EBIT/FCF inflection and a guidance-beat streak. That same agent, applying MODULE_RULES' own §24 Filter-1 cap, reduces its reportable score to 60 because of the unresolved derivative lawsuit (RF-MGT-005). This synthesis carries the capped 60, not the raw 71 — the operating record is real, but it does not buy back the credit lost to an unresolved integrity allegation naming the CEO directly, per the module's own rule.
2. **Structurally clean board (05: 69/100, "adequate" not "strong") vs. thin ownership and no incentive visibility (03/04: Insufficient Data / 59/100).** Agent 05 finds a well-formed governance box — 90% independent, split chair/CEO, no promoter, no pledging — but caps its own verdict at "adequate" specifically because the same board is named in the unresolved lawsuit. Agents 03 and 04 find the people this board oversees have almost no personal capital riding on the stock (0.18% insider ownership, no identifiable cash buying) and that incentive design cannot be checked at all because the proxy is missing. Reconciled view: procedural independence does not substitute for evidenced alignment or for a board's oversight function actually being tested and passing — a board can be structurally sound on paper and still be the subject of a live, unresolved question about whether that oversight was real. Shareholder friendliness is capped at 60, not credited with 05's own 69.

**Forensic tag propagation (CLAUDE.md §13; eval check AQ).** `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md` fired all three of the following as standalone lines, propagated here: **RF-DISC-001** (management continued making quantified guidance-beat claims on an Adjusted EBITDA metric whose GAAP reconciliation the company quietly stopped publishing starting Q1 FY2026 — High materiality), **RF-DISC-002** (a "legal, non-income tax, and regulatory reserve" line recurred at $1,123M FY2024 / $564M FY2025 despite its "non-recurring" framing — High materiality), and **RF-REG-002** (two adjudicated MDL jury verdicts, Feb-2026 and Apr-2026, were not named in the three consecutive filings that followed them — Medium materiality). These three tags, together with `01`'s and `05`'s independently-fired **RF-MGT-005** and `02`'s **RF-CAP-004**, should feed the master synthesizer's cross-module forensic roll-up alongside `earnings/06`'s RF-EQ-001/RF-EQ-002 and `balance-sheet-survival/05`'s RF-OBS-001 to check for a compounding accounting-integrity mosaic that no single module's own score cap would catch alone.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Yes and no, with a critical caveat neither side can waive away. Management has delivered on the specific promises it makes — four straight quarters of Adjusted EBITDA beaten against its own guided range, two of them above the guided high end — and the four-year EBIT/FCF trajectory (FY2021–FY2025: EBIT margin −22.0% to +10.7%; FCF −$743M to +$9,763M) shows capital deployed into the core business has genuinely created per-share value. That record does not extend to M&A: of the deals with a knowable outcome, Drizly ($1.1bn) was a full write-off within 2.5 years and Careem was sold to a minority stake in 2023 only to be bought back to control by mid/late-2026, and the team is now committing $14.8bn — funded mostly by new debt — to Delivery Hero, the largest and least-proven capital-allocation decision in the company's history. Incentives cannot be assessed at all: the 10-K defers every comp detail to a 2026 proxy statement that is not in the data pool, so whether pay rewards per-share value or size is a genuine unknown, not a clean bill of health. Insiders own a rounding error of the company (0.18%) with no identifiable open-market cash purchase in six months. Minority shareholders sit inside a structurally clean-looking board (90% independent, split chair/CEO, no promoter, no pledging) that nonetheless carries a disclosed basket of entrenchment tools and — the single most important fact governing this entire read — is now the subject of an unresolved federal derivative lawsuit alleging the board's own recent proxy statements about safety oversight were false. That allegation is unproven. But it names the exact people this report is judging, it directly tests whether the board's independence is real oversight or box-ticking, and this module's own rules do not allow it to be averaged away against the genuinely strong operating record sitting next to it.

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict: Serious governance concerns.** This is a rule-driven cap, not a finding of proven fraud: two Critical-materiality red flags fired in this module (RF-MGT-005, an unresolved shareholder derivative lawsuit naming the sitting CEO, Chairman, and every director; RF-CAP-004, the serial-acquirer pattern culminating in the $14.8bn debt-funded Delivery Hero bid), and MODULE_RULES.md requires that any Critical red flag caps the verdict "no better than 'Serious governance concerns'" regardless of the composite score or how clean the underlying board mechanics look.
- **Capital-allocation record: mixed, capped at 50/100.** Organic reinvestment is genuinely value-creative (~29% estimated incremental ROIC, caveated; capex held to 0.65% of revenue) and buybacks have reduced net share count for the first time in the company's history over the most recent 15 months. M&A has been value-destructive on the observable evidence (Drizly full write-off, Careem sold-then-re-bought-to-control), and the pending $14.8bn Delivery Hero deal — funded mostly by a €14.2bn bridge that would roughly double gross debt if drawn — is the largest and least-proven capital-allocation decision in the record.
- **Incentives and ownership: cannot confirm alignment with minority holders.** Comp metrics/weights are Insufficient Data (2026 proxy missing from pool); insiders hold 0.18% of the company with zero identifiable open-market cash purchases in six months and modest net selling. Do not read this absence of red flags as a clean bill of health — it is a genuine unknown, not a clearance.
- **Biggest governance risk / red flag: RF-MGT-005 (Critical) — an unresolved federal shareholder derivative lawsuit** names the sitting CEO, Chairman, and every sitting director, alleging the board ignored an internal safety-risk model for five years (2017–2022) and that its own 2024–2026 proxy statements about board safety oversight were false. This is unproven (confidence 2/5, web-sourced only) but was independently flagged by three specialists (01, 05, 06) this run — a materially more cautious read than a prior run's characterization of the same underlying lawsuit as "cleared." Close second: RF-CAP-004 (Critical), the serial-acquirer / debt-funded-Delivery-Hero pattern.
- **Hard disqualifier flagged by `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`: None** — verbatim: "No hard disqualifier found in the data pool... 0 of 8 tests triggered; 0 of 5 near-misses in band." That scan separately routed the derivative lawsuit to this module as an unadjudicated integrity signal (not a proven-fraud hard lock); this module's own Critical-red-flag rule is what caps the verdict here, not the disqualifier scan.
- **§24 rejector filters tripped:** Filter 1 (integrity, RF-MGT-005) — Management quality capped max 60, Disclosure candor cap max 60 (non-binding). Filter 4 (serial acquirer, RF-CAP-004) — Capital allocation capped max 50, Governance risk floored 60. Filters 2 (turnaround) and 6 (unaligned owner) were tested and NOT tripped — the 2017-era governance turnaround shows 4 delivered years of proof, and no controlling owner exists.
- **Partial-data caps applied:** Incentive alignment (Insufficient Data; hard cap max 50, non-binding since the actual evidence-based score is 5); Shareholder friendliness (capped at 60 from a blended ~64, on the missing ownership/insider-transaction and beneficial-ownership data); Overall usefulness (capped at 70, actual 68). No caps applied to board disclosure or the base capital-allocation methodology — each had sufficient primary evidence independent of the §24 conviction caps.
- **Biggest missing data point:** The 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement — it would simultaneously resolve the compensation-metric, beneficial-ownership-percentage, insider-transaction-history, RPT-policy, and AGM-vote-tally gaps, and is the single document every relevant 10-K item (Items 10–14) points to by reference. (This is a genuine document gap, not a language-translation issue — Uber is a US SEC filer and the entire filing set in this pool is in English.)
- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance` quick-read. The master synthesizer should treat THIS module's governance verdict (Serious governance concerns), Governance Score (43/100), Confidence-Adjusted Score (26/100), and Governance Rating (Weak) as the primary governance read for UBER — not the business-model module's own quick-read score.

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management keeps its word on the numbers it actually guides — Adjusted EBITDA has beaten guidance four quarters running, twice above the high end — and four straight years of profit and cash-flow improvement back that up.
- Money spent building the actual business earns strong returns; money spent buying other companies has mostly not worked — a $1.1bn alcohol-delivery deal (Drizly) was shut down entirely, and a $3.1bn Middle East deal (Careem) was sold down and then bought back to control within three years.
- Executive pay cannot be checked at all — the document that would show it (the 2026 proxy) is missing from the data pool.
- Insiders own about 0.18% of the company and have not bought any stock with their own cash in the last six months; they are modest net sellers on what looks like a routine schedule.
- Minority shareholders sit behind a board that looks genuinely independent on paper (90% independent, split chair/CEO) — but that same board is now the target of an unresolved federal lawsuit alleging it ignored internal safety warnings for years and that its own recent statements about safety oversight were false.
- Uber quietly stopped publishing the math behind the profit number it keeps beating, and left two adjudicated lawsuit verdicts out of its filings — real candor concerns, even though nothing checked against a primary filing was found to be false.
- No hard governance disqualifier was flagged — no fraud finding, no pledging, no runaway related-party dealing, no auditor issue — but the unresolved lawsuit is serious enough, under this module's own rules, to cap the whole verdict at "serious governance concerns" until it resolves.
- This module is useful but incomplete for the master synthesizer: the board-structure, capital-allocation, and candor reads are solid; the compensation and precise-ownership reads are not assessable until the 2026 proxy is added to the data pool.

RF-MGT-005,"Unresolved shareholder derivative lawsuit naming CEO, Chairman, and every sitting director, alleging suppressed safety-risk data and false 2024-2026 proxy disclosures (Section 24 Filter 1)",Critical,"Complaint filed N.D. Cal. (reported Jun-22/Jul-23-2026); alleges a 2017-built ML safety-risk model not deployed until 2022; alleges >400000 assault-adjacent incidents 2017-2022 vs ~12522 disclosed; no MTD ruling or settlement on record","Web: Forbes 2026-07-23 (unverified); Web: TechCrunch 2026-06-22 (unverified)","Management quality capped max 60; Disclosure candor cap max 60 (non-binding); forces Governance Rating no better than Weak and verdict no better than Serious governance concerns","Track docket for motion-to-dismiss ruling, discovery, or settlement"
RF-CAP-004,"Serial-acquirer / debt-funded M&A pattern (Section 24 Filter 4): Careem sold to minority 2023 then re-bought to control 2026; pending 14.8bn debt-funded Delivery Hero takeover, bridge ~1.2x pre-deal total debt",Critical,"14.8bn USD equity value, 14.2bn EUR (15.3bn USD) senior-unsecured bridge signed Jul-16-2026; would roughly double gross debt if drawn; ~8-10% of Uber's own market cap","Q2 FY26 10-Q Note 3, filed 2026-08-05; FY25 10-K Note 18, filed 2026-02-13","Capital allocation capped max 50; Governance risk floor 60","Track deal financing terms, closing conditions (exp. H2 2027), leverage-covenant disclosure"
RF-DISC-001,"Discontinuation of the company-level Adjusted-EBITDA-to-GAAP reconciliation, effective Q1 FY2026, unacknowledged on either subsequent earnings call",High,"Zero mentions of Adjusted EBITDA in Q1 or Q2 FY2026 transcripts (full-text search); reconciliation disclosed quarterly through FY2025","Q1/Q2 FY26 10-Q MD&A (2026-05-06 / 2026-08-05); Q1/Q2 FY26 transcripts","Weighs down Management quality and Disclosure candor","Confirm whether the reconciliation returns in the FY2026 10-K"
RF-DISC-002,"Recurring legal/non-income-tax/regulatory reserve line added back to Adjusted EBITDA as non-recurring in both FY2024 (1123M) and FY2025 (564M)",High,"Present at material size two years running, both times framed as an adjustment","FY25 10-K Item 7, filed 2026-02-13; earnings/06_earnings-quality.md","Weighs down Disclosure candor","Confirm whether this line recurs a third year in FY2026"
RF-CAP-001,"Drizly (1.1bn alcohol-delivery acquisition, closed Oct-2021) shut down entirely ~2.5 years after close",High,"Full write-off of the deal's strategic rationale, not merely underperformance","Web: CBS News/CNN/PYMNTS 2024-01-15/01-16 (unverified); FY25 10-K risk factors","Weighs down Capital allocation","Confirm any residual goodwill impairment tied to Drizly in FY2024 10-K notes"
RF-REG-002,"Two adjudicated MDL jury verdicts (Arizona 8.5mm Feb-2026; Charlotte NC 5000 Apr-2026) not named in the three most recent filings despite an aggregate litigation reserve disclosed",Medium,"Specific, checkable disclosure-timeliness gap on adjudicated, publicly known verdicts","FY25 10-K; Q1/Q2 FY26 10-Q; carried red_flags.csv","Weighs down Disclosure candor","Check next 10-Q/10-K for MDL naming"

Because Uber is a US SEC filer, the canonical source is the DEF 14A proxy — not a local-market equivalent — and it is genuinely absent from this pool (not merely a naming mismatch). This is a true US-regime gap, not a jurisdiction-detection error.

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict:** Partial
- **Reason:** Board composition, related-party figures, control structure, multi-year capital-allocation history, management-turnover events, and two current earnings-call transcripts are all present and usable, but the proxy/DEF 14A that would carry individual executive-compensation tables (metrics and weights), the beneficial-ownership percentage table, and Section 16 insider-transaction data is genuinely absent from the pool — a true gap for a US SEC filer, not a jurisdiction-mapping error. The 10-K's own Items 11–14 confirm this by deferring every comp/ownership/related-party item to the "2026 Proxy Statement," which was not ingested.
- **Specialists that can run:** management track record (01), capital allocation (02), board and shareholder rights (05, on the data that is present), candor (06) — all with the pool's own filings, transcripts, and the CIQ dossiers. Incentives (03) and ownership/insider behavior (04) can run but are capped per MODULE_RULES.md because their primary source document is missing.
- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan?** N — no disqualifier triggered (all 8 tests: N; 0 of 5 near-misses in band). Two soft, unproven integrity signals were routed to this module for context, not as a verdict-lock trigger: (1) a discontinued consolidated Adjusted-EBITDA-to-GAAP reconciliation (starting Q1 FY2026) alongside continued guidance-beat claims, flagged as a disclosure-candor issue in `earnings/06`/`earnings/08`, not a proven misstatement; (2) an unadjudicated shareholder derivative lawsuit (filed Jul-23-2026, N.D. Cal.) naming the CEO, Chairman, all sitting directors, and two former CFOs, alleging the board ignored an internal ML safety-incident risk model — confidence 2/5, unadjudicated. Neither meets the proven-fraud bar for a hard lock; both should be weighed by the management-track-record and candor specialists (RF-MGT-005 territory).
- **Active partial-data caps:**
  - No proxy / compensation disclosure → Incentive alignment (03) max 50; Overall usefulness (99) max 70.
  - No ownership / insider-transaction data → Shareholder friendliness (04/99 read) max 60.
- **Critical missing items:**
  - 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement (executive compensation tables and metric weights, beneficial-ownership percentage table, Section 16 insider-transaction history, RPT policy narrative, AGM vote-result tallies) — not in `data/UBER/`.
  - Standalone 8-K filings for the CFO transition (Feb-2026) and CPO/President reorganization (May-2026) — only available secondhand via the CIQ Key Developments vendor feed, not the primary SEC filing.
- **Single highest-value missing document:** The 2026 DEF 14A Proxy Statement (or, failing that, the prior-year DEF 14A) — it would resolve the compensation-metric, beneficial-ownership, and insider-transaction gaps simultaneously and is the single document Items 10–14 of the FY25 10-K point to by reference.

# Management & Track Record — UBER

## 1. Key Executives

**Founder / owner-operator status:** The CEO is **not** a founder. Dara Khosrowshahi was hired from Expedia in September 2017 specifically to replace ousted co-founder Travis Kalanick following Uber's 2017 governance and workplace-culture crisis [CIQ Key Developments, Arizona MDL trial witness-list reference naming "co-founder and former CEO" Kalanick alongside "current CEO Dara Khosrowshahi," 2026]. Uber has a **single class of common stock** (no dual-class / super-voting structure), a float of 99.7% of shares outstanding (2,042.6mm shares out), and **no controlling shareholder or promoter block** — 1,207 holders of record [management-governance/00_governance-data-triage.md, §Control structure; FY25 10-K, Item 5, p.5289]. Co-founder Garrett Camp holds only a non-voting Board Observer role since May 2020 and has no executive function. This is a professionally-managed public company with no founder-control overhang.

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last ~12 months) | Four C-suite/president-level changes: (1) CFO Prashanth Mahendra-Rajah stepped down Feb-16-2026 (explicitly stated as not accounting-related); (2) Chief People Officer Nikki Krishnamurthy stepped down May-11-2026; (3) CTO role restructured Dec-2025 (Praveen Neppalli Naga elevated from "CTO, Mobility & Delivery" since Nov-2024 to company-wide CTO); (4) Uber Freight (majority-owned logistics subsidiary) founder-CEO Lior Ron transitioned to Chairman, replaced by internal Uber executive Rebecca Tinucci (Sept-2025) — described by the company as "a new chapter," not a distress exit; Ron moved to COO of Waabi (a related AV company) | CIQ Key Developments (CFO, "Executive Changes Effective May 11 2026," Uber Freight CEO items); CIQ Professionals export |
| CFO turnover | One CFO change in the trailing 12 months (Feb-2026), the first since Nelson Chai's tenure ended and Mahendra-Rajah's began (Mahendra-Rajah's own start date is not in this pool). The replacement is a 6-year internal veteran (joined 2019) rather than an outside hire — reduces (but does not eliminate) execution-continuity risk. The company's own statement disclaims any accounting-disagreement cause, which this agent treats as a disclosed fact, not independently verified | CIQ Key Developments, 2026-02-16 announcement |
| CEO succession / continuity | No CEO change. Khosrowshahi has led the company continuously since Sep-2017 (~8.9 years), spanning the pre-IPO turnaround, the 2019 IPO, and the shift to sustained GAAP/cash profitability. This is a long, stable CEO tenure by any base rate | CIQ Professionals export; `01_historical-financials.md` (financial trajectory under this CEO) |
| Regional leadership churn | Uber India head Prabhjeet Singh resigned to join an unrelated "frontier tech" company; several new regional marketing/BD hires made in 2026 (Head of Marketing India, Head of Advertising India) | CIQ Key Developments |

**Read:** No CEO or Chairman turnover — the top of the house is stable. But the pace of change one level down (CFO, Chief People Officer, CTO scope, and a large subsidiary's CEO, all within roughly a 12-month window) is a real signal, even though every one of these was an orderly internal promotion with no stated cause for concern. Four senior transitions in a year at a company of Uber's scale is above what a "steady state" leadership team would show — flagged as a monitoring item, not a red flag on its own, because none carries disclosed evidence of disagreement, malfeasance, or forced exit.

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management describes Uber's strategy as continuing to compound Gross Bookings growth (Trips × Monthly Active Platform Consumers) across Mobility, Delivery, and Freight while expanding the take rate and converting a growing share of Adjusted EBITDA into free cash flow, layering in advertising and Uber One subscription revenue as incremental, largely-margin-accretive revenue streams, and building an autonomous-vehicle (AV) partnership network (Waymo, WeRide, Nvidia, Pony.ai and others) rather than developing self-driving technology in-house [Q2 FY26 transcript, prepared remarks; `business-model/02_business-identity.md`]. The most specific, checkable numeric targets on record are the **February 2024 Investor Day three-year (FY2024–FY2026) framework**: Gross Bookings growth of mid-to-high-teens % CAGR (constant currency), Adjusted EBITDA growth of high-30s-to-40% CAGR, and free-cash-flow conversion of 90%+ of Adjusted EBITDA [Web: Uber Investor Update, Feb-2024, hosted on Uber's own IR site (s23.q4cdn.com), and multiple contemporaneous financial-media reports (Seeking Alpha, "Uber gains after setting aggressive three-year targets") — dated, company-sourced investor material, not in this data pool, labelled unverified per this module's web-source rule]. On a shorter horizon, Uber guides quarterly (adjusted) EBITDA and, since Q1 FY2026, EPS Normalized — it does not issue formal Revenue, Capex, or GAAP-Net-Income guidance [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, §2].

## 4. Promise vs Delivery

| Prior Promise / Target | Period Set | Actual Outcome | Kept / Missed | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gross Bookings growth: mid-to-high-teens % CAGR (constant currency), FY2024–FY2026 | Set Feb-2024 (3-yr outlook) | FY2025 Gross Bookings +19% reported / +20% constant-currency [`business-model/02_business-identity.md`, §4] | **Kept** — at or above the top of the guided range | Web: Feb-2024 Investor Update (unverified, company-sourced) vs FY25 10-K (pool) |
| Adjusted EBITDA growth: high-30s-to-40% CAGR, FY2024–FY2026 | Set Feb-2024 (3-yr outlook) | FY2024→FY2025 Adjusted EBITDA growth: $6,484M → $8,730M = +34.6% [`business-model/02_business-identity.md`, §2; FY25 10-K, p.49] | **Mostly kept** — one year's growth (34.6%) sits just below the low end of the "high-30s to 40%" range; full 3-year CAGR not independently computable from this pool (no FY2023 Adjusted EBITDA figure in pool) | Web: Feb-2024 Investor Update (unverified) vs FY25 10-K (pool) |
| Free cash flow conversion: 90%+ of Adjusted EBITDA, FY2024–FY2026 | Set Feb-2024 (3-yr outlook) | FY2025: FCF $9,763M ÷ Adjusted EBITDA $8,730M = 111.8% conversion [`01_historical-financials.md`, §1; FY25 10-K, Item 7] | **Kept, exceeded** | Web (target) vs `earnings/01_historical-financials.md` (pool, outcome) |

**Read:** On the metrics management actually commits numbers to — the 2024 Investor Day 3-year framework and quarterly EBITDA guidance — the record is strong: two of three Investor Day targets clearly cleared, the third close; and EBITDA guidance beaten four straight quarters, with the beat margin widening (above the high end, not just the midpoint) in the two most recent prints. Revenue, which is not formally guided, has narrowly missed consensus for two straight quarters, with a credible one-off explanation for the larger part of the gap. This is a genuine, checkable kept-promises record, not narrative.

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | **Historically yes, but already delivered, not a live thesis.** The 2017 Khosrowshahi-for-Kalanick CEO change was a governance-and-culture turnaround following Uber's 2017 workplace-culture crisis. By 2026 this is 8+ years old and the operating turnaround has been substantially completed and evidenced in the numbers, not merely promised | CIQ Key Developments (2017 CEO change context via trial witness list); `01_historical-financials.md` |
| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | **4 consecutive years of delivered improvement** (FY2021→FY2025): EBIT margin −22.0% → −5.7% → +3.0% → +6.4% → +10.7%; FCF −$743M → +$390M → +$3,362M → +$6,895M → +$9,763M. This clears the ≥2–3-year bar with room to spare | `earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §1 |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | No — the current record rests on 4+ years of filed, audited financial results (EBIT, FCF, Adjusted EBITDA, guidance-beat streak), not on Khosrowshahi's Expedia résumé or a forward-looking deck. The base-rate penalty in Filter 2 (star-CEO résumé as false comfort) does **not** apply here because the proof standard is met with delivered numbers | `01_historical-financials.md`; `earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md` |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (unverified adverse signal) | **Yes — chased, not cleared.** Two items routed: (1) `RF-DISC-001` — the consolidated Adjusted-EBITDA-to-GAAP reconciliation was discontinued starting Q1 FY2026 even as management continued to make guidance-beat claims, a disclosure-candor concern (not proven misstatement); (2) an **unadjudicated shareholder derivative lawsuit** filed in federal court (N.D. Cal.) around Jun-22/reported Jul-23, 2026, brought by a Detroit pension fund, naming CEO Dara Khosrowshahi, Chairman Ronald Sugar, other sitting directors, the president, and two former CFOs. The complaint alleges Uber built an internal machine-learning tool in 2017 that could predict rider-assault risk with ~51% confidence using 43 risk factors, did not deploy it until 2022, continued dispatching flagged high-risk trips without warning riders in the interim, and that the board's 2024–2026 proxy disclosures about safety oversight were false; it alleges >400,000 assault-adjacent incidents 2017–2022 against a smaller disclosed figure (~12,522 in the company's own Safety Report for the same window, per the carried prior-run red-flag record). This is a **serious, unresolved allegation against the sitting CEO and full board**, filed only weeks before this report's as-of date, with no motion-to-dismiss ruling, discovery outcome, or settlement yet on record | `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, §"Integrity note"; management-governance carried `red_flags.csv` (`UNREGISTERED-BOARD-OVERSIGHT`, prior-run artifact, confidence 2/5, unadjudicated); Web: Forbes, "New Lawsuit Claims Uber Covered Up Sexual Assault Stats, Board 'Ignored Red Flags,'" 2026-07-23 (unverified, dated); Web: TechCrunch, "Shareholders sue Uber's board over sexual assaults, other incidents," 2026-06-22 (unverified, dated) |

**Turnaround rule (Filter 2) applied.** Because Uber's governance turnaround shows ≥2–3 years of *delivered* operating inflection (in fact 4), it is **not** penalized under the base-rate turnaround discount, and `RF-MGT-004` is **not** triggered. It is still worth naming honestly: this was originally a governance-turnaround thesis (2017), and the proof standard that clears it is exactly the standard §24 demands — years of numbers, not a pitch.

**Integrity rule (Filter 1) applied.** The shareholder derivative lawsuit is unproven and was investigated as far as this data pool and a dated web search allow. It is not discarded because Uber's audit opinion is clean and no disqualifier-scan hard trigger fired (§13/§24 distinguish proven fraud, which is a hard lock owned by the disqualifier scan, from unproven-but-unignorable signal, which this agent owns). Because the signal remains **unresolved** — neither cleared by primary evidence (no dismissal, no settlement, no board investigation outcome in this pool) nor escalated to a proven fact — this agent emits the standalone red-flag line below and applies the conviction cap.

**RF-MGT-005 (unresolved adverse integrity signal, unproven)**

## 5. Management Read

Dara Khosrowshahi has run Uber for nearly nine years, not as a founder but as the executive brought in to clean up a governance crisis — and the numbers back up that he did it: EBIT and free cash flow improved every year for four straight years (FY2021–FY2025), and the company has beaten its own quarterly Adjusted EBITDA guidance in four consecutive quarters, clearing the top of the guided range (not just the midpoint) in the two most recent prints. Beneath the CEO, the bench has turned over quickly — four C-suite/president-level changes in roughly a year, all internal promotions with no disclosed cause for concern, which is a stability watch item rather than a red flag on its own. The single biggest management signal, however, is negative and unresolved: a shareholder derivative lawsuit filed weeks before this report names the sitting CEO and entire board, alleging they sat on a safety-risk model for five years (2017–2022) and that recent proxy statements about board safety oversight were false. That allegation is unproven, but it is serious, it names the people this report is judging, and — combined with the company's own decision to stop reconciling its headline Adjusted EBITDA metric to GAAP starting this year — it caps how much credit this agent can give management on candor and integrity until the litigation resolves.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — UBER

Reporting currency: USD. Reporting standard: US GAAP. Fiscal year end: December 31. Figures below cover FY2021–FY2025 (5 fiscal years) plus, where flagged, the trailing twelve months (LTM) through Jun-30-2026 and subsequent events through the 2026-08-09 as-of date [FY25 10-K, filed 2026-02-13; Q1 FY26 10-Q, filed 2026-05-06; Q2 FY26 10-Q, filed 2026-08-05]. This module runs before valuation, so buyback price discipline below is judged only against observable evidence (trading range, quarter-end closes, leverage trend) — not against intrinsic or fair value.

## 1. Uses of Capital (FY2021–FY2025, cumulative)

| Use | Cumulative Amount ($M) | Currency | % of CFO (cum. $21,018M) | % of FCF (cum. $19,667M) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|
| Acquisitions (cash, gross) | 3,188 | USD | 15.2% | 16.2% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Cash Acquisitions" row: FY2021 −2,314; FY2022 −59; FY2023 0; FY2024 0; FY2025 −815] |
| Buybacks | 7,775 | USD | 37.0% | 39.5% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Repurchase of Common Stock": FY2024 −1,252; FY2025 −6,523; FY2021–FY2023 = 0] |
| Dividends | 0 | USD | 0.0% | 0.0% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Total Dividends Paid" = 0 in every year FY2016–LTM] |
| Capex | 1,351 | USD | 6.4% | 6.9% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Capital Expenditure": 298+252+223+242+336] |
| Debt repayment (gross) | 10,617 | USD | 50.5% | 54.0% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Total Debt Repaid" (absolute): 842+264+2,846+4,158+2,507] |
| Debt issued (gross, memo) | 11,921 | USD | 56.7% | 60.6% | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Total Debt Issued": 1,766+0+2,824+3,972+3,359] |
| Net debt issuance (memo) | +1,304 | USD | 6.2% | 6.6% | Debt issued minus debt repaid, above |

Cumulative CFO FY2021–FY2025 = $21,018M (−445+642+3,585+7,137+10,099); cumulative FCF (CFO − |capex|) = $19,667M [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §1]. Debt repayment and issuance both include ordinary refinancing of maturing notes/term loans, not only net deleveraging — the "net debt issuance" memo row (+$1,304M) is the number that matters for capital-structure intent. **This table excludes the pending Delivery Hero SE takeover** (agreement signed Jul-16-2026, subsequent event) and the pending Getir acquisition (agreement signed Feb-8-2026, also a subsequent event) — both are forward-looking commitments, not cash actually deployed in FY2021–FY2025; they are scored separately in Section 2 because they are the single largest capital-allocation decision in the company's history and cannot be excluded from the verdict just because the cash has not yet moved.

**Read:** buybacks (37% of cumulative CFO) are now Uber's largest actual use of capital in this window, ahead of acquisitions (15%) and capex (6%) — Uber pays no dividend at all. Debt was, on net, a small source of cash (+$1,304M net issuance) over FY2021–FY2025, funding part of the FY2025 buyback acceleration; that pattern intensified sharply in H1 FY2026 (see Section 3).

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| Careem (Middle East ride-hailing + non-ridesharing "Careem Technologies") | Announced Mar-2019, closed Jan-2020 | $3.1bn ($1.4bn cash + up to $1.7bn convertible notes) | Consolidate the Middle East/North Africa ride-hailing market; "super app" ambitions | **Value markedly written down.** In Dec-2023 Uber divested majority control of Careem Technologies (the non-ridesharing unit) to Emirates Telecom (e&) for e& injecting $400M for majority ownership; Uber's total consideration received was $340M (fair value of shares $300M + cash $40M) against a $136M carrying value, booking a $204M gain on that carve-out alone — a fraction of the original $3.1bn deal price. Uber then began re-acquiring Careem Technologies stake through 2026: ownership rose from the post-2023 ~42% to ~45% by Jun-30-2026, and on Jul-30-2026 (subsequent event) Uber obtained a **controlling interest** again through a further purchase — reversing the 2023 divestiture within under 3 years. Separately, in Jun-2026 Uber agreed to buy a further 12.5% Careem Technologies stake from e& for $100M cash, implying a ~$800M valuation for the whole non-ridesharing unit versus the $3.1bn 2019 deal price for the combined entity | [Forbes/CNBC, 2019-03-26, unverified web]; [FY25 10-K, Note 18 – Divestitures, p.121]; [Q2 FY26 10-Q, Note 3 – Investments, "Careem Technologies" section (Jun-30-2026 ownership ~45%; Jul-30-2026 controlling-interest purchase)]; [CIQ Key Developments, "acquire 12.50% stake in Careem Technologies... for $100 million," dated ~Jun-2026] |
| Postmates (US food delivery) | Announced Jul-2020, closed Dec-2020 | $2.65bn, all-stock (~84mm Uber shares at a fixed $31.45/share) | Add scale and market share in US food delivery via Uber Eats | **Not separately trackable — absorbed into the Uber Eats brand.** The Postmates app and brand still operate in 2026 (referenced in 2026 retailer-partnership press releases alongside "Uber Eats" and "Uber"), but Uber discloses no standalone financial or synergy tracking for the Postmates deal in this pool; no independent post-acquisition return is disclosed | [Web: CNBC/TechCrunch, 2020-07-06/2020-12-01, unverified]; [CIQ Key Developments, 2026 retailer-partnership items referencing the "Postmates app" still in use] |
| Cornershop (Latin America grocery delivery) | Announced Oct-2019 (51% stake, $459M), later taken to 100% | 2019–2021 | Enter grocery delivery in Mexico/Chile | **No disclosed write-off; outcome not independently trackable.** FY25 10-K risk factors still list Cornershop as an operating, majority/wholly-owned brand; no standalone return data disclosed | [Web: CB Insights, 2019, unverified]; [FY25 10-K, Item 1A risk factors, p. (Cornershop referenced as a brand operating alongside Careem/Postmates)] |
| The Drizly Group (US alcohol delivery) | Acquired Oct-2021 (closed) | $1.1bn | Expand Delivery offering into alcohol | **Failed — shut down entirely.** Uber closed Drizly in Mar-2024, roughly 2.5 years after closing the deal, redirecting the alcohol-delivery use case back into the core Uber Eats app. This is a full write-off of the strategic rationale, not merely an underperformance | [FY25 10-K, Item 1A risk factors, p. ("in October 2021, we acquired The Drizly Group, Inc. in order to further expand our Delivery offering to alcohol")]; [Web: CBS News/CNN/PYMNTS, 2024-01-15/01-16, unverified — "Uber to shut down Drizly, the alcohol delivery service it bought for $1.1 billion"] |
| Transplace (Uber Freight — transportation-management software) | Announced Jul-2021, closed Nov-2021 | $2.25bn (up to $750M in Uber stock, remainder cash; partly financed by $550M of outside minority capital into Uber Freight) | Expand Uber Freight's transportation-management capability | **Not clearly delivering — Freight remains structurally weak.** Uber Freight's founder-CEO transitioned out of the role in Sept-2025 (replaced by an internal Uber executive, described by the company as "a new chapter," not framed as a distress exit) [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, §2]; Freight is independently flagged elsewhere in this pipeline as loss-making and cyclical [`business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`, segment-map row]. No disclosed standalone ROI for the Transplace deal in this pool | [Web: Dallas Innovates/SiliconANGLE/TechCrunch, 2021-07-22/11-15, unverified]; [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, §2]; [`business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md`] |
| Foodpanda Taiwan (from Delivery Hero) | Announced May-2024, closing phased into 2025 | $950M cash + a separate $300M investment in newly issued Delivery Hero shares | Consolidate the Taiwan food-delivery market; build a commercial relationship with Delivery Hero | **Escalated, not resolved — became the seed of the FY2026 full takeover bid.** The $300M 2024 stake grew (through further purchases and a $1.6bn Total-Return-Swap position on Delivery Hero stock taken in Q2 FY2026) into a $3.1bn equity-method investment (carrying value $3.5bn) by Jun-30-2026, immediately followed by the Jul-16-2026 full takeover agreement — a minority stake used as a toehold for a much larger, debt-funded deal | [Web: Bloomberg/CNBC/TechCrunch, 2024-05-14, unverified]; [Q2 FY26 10-Q, Note 3 – Investments, "Delivery Hero" equity-method section] |
| Trendyol GO (Türkiye online meal/grocery delivery, 85% stake) | Closed Jun-17-2025 | $694M, all-cash | Expand Delivery segment presence in Türkiye | **Too recent to assess.** Contributed "an immaterial amount of revenue and loss before taxes" for the Jun–Dec 2025 stub period disclosed; goodwill of $712M was recorded (>100% of the deal's net-asset fair value), meaning the price paid was overwhelmingly for expected synergies/workforce, not tangible or identifiable intangible assets | [FY25 10-K, Note 17 – Business Combinations, p.120–121] |
| Getir food-delivery business (Türkiye, from Mubadala) | Agreement signed Feb-8-2026 (subsequent event); expected close H2 2026 | $435M cash, plus a minority interest in Getir's grocery business | Further consolidate the Turkish delivery market alongside Trendyol GO | **Pending — not yet closed as of this report's as-of date.** | [FY25 10-K, Note 19 – Subsequent Events, p.121] |
| **Delivery Hero SE (full takeover)** | Agreement signed Jul-16-2026; expected close H2 2027 | **$14.8bn equity value** (€41.50/share cash offer for 100%), funded via existing cash + a **€14.2bn ($15.3bn) senior-unsecured bridge credit facility** executed the same day | Consolidate global food-delivery competitor Delivery Hero; extend Uber Eats/Delivery scale globally | **Pending — the single largest, highest-stakes capital-allocation decision in Uber's history; no return evidence exists yet.** The bridge facility alone (€14.2bn) is roughly 1.2x Uber's entire pre-deal debt stack ($12.6bn total indebtedness at Jun-30-2026) and about 8–10% of Uber's own market capitalization (~$139–161bn range across recent quarters). If drawn in full, gross debt would roughly double | [Q2 FY26 10-Q, Note 3 – Investments, "Pending Acquisition of Delivery Hero"]; [`business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`, §1] |

**Serial-acquirer + opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4) — applied.** Uber has run a near-continuous acquisition program since 2019 (at least eight material deals: Careem, Postmates, Cornershop, Drizly, Transplace, Foodpanda Taiwan, Trendyol GO, Getir) and is now pursuing the largest deal in its history (Delivery Hero, $14.8bn equity value) financed by a debt bridge that would roughly double gross indebtedness. Of the deals with a knowable outcome: **one was a full write-off (Drizly, closed and shut down within 2.5 years)**; **one was substantially reversed (Careem — divested to minority in 2023, then re-acquired to a controlling stake by mid/late-2026, implying the original $3.1bn strategic thesis was abandoned and then partially re-bought at a fraction of the price)**; and the Foodpanda Taiwan and pending Getir/Trendyol GO deals show no disclosed synergy realization yet. Charging the pattern its opportunity cost (per CLAUDE.md §24, not just each deal's own reported return): Uber has repeatedly redirected capital and management attention into new verticals (alcohol, freight software, Latin American grocery, Turkish delivery, and now German-listed global food delivery) rather than compounding around its core Mobility/Delivery platform, and the Drizly/Careem reversals show real capital was destroyed and strategic focus was lost, not merely "delayed synergies." Bare rationale language ("expand our Delivery offering," "further consolidate the market") is not evidence of value creation on its own — it is scored as such here.

**RF-CAP-004 triggered** (serial-acquirer / value-destructive M&A pattern, debt-funded deal near/above own value) — consistent with the upstream `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` finding, which this module's own deal-level review corroborates and deepens rather than merely repeats.

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

*Buyback discipline below is judged only on observable evidence (trading range, quarter-end price, leverage trend, SBC offset) — not against intrinsic/fair value, which this module does not assess.*

| Year | Amount Spent ($M) | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change (period-end) | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2021–FY2023 | 0 | USD | 0 | n/a | Shares out. rose 1,954.5mm (Dec-21) → 2,076.5mm (Dec-23), +6.2% | No — no buybacks in the window; SBC alone drove +6.2% dilution | n/a — no repurchase activity | No buyback activity; all growth-phase capital went to acquisitions/opex | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Historical Capitalization; Cash Flow] |
| FY2024 | 1,252 | USD | Not disclosed at share-count granularity in this pool | Not determinable from this pool (dollars disclosed; shares repurchased not separately disclosed) | Shares out. rose 2,076.5mm (Dec-23) → 2,089.0mm (Dec-24), +0.6% (buybacks began late in the year, small relative to SBC-driven issuance) | **No** — SBC expense alone was $1,796M in FY2024, exceeding the $1,252M spent on buybacks; net share count still rose | Insufficient granularity to price FY2024 buybacks against the trading range | Buybacks partially offset but did not overcome FY2024 dilution | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow ("Repurchase of Common Stock" −1,252; "Stock-Based Compensation" 1,796); Historical Capitalization] |
| FY2025 | 6,523 (incl. a $1.5bn Q1 Accelerated Share Repurchase) | USD | Q4 2025 only: 21.369mm shares (Oct 5.746mm + Nov 9.689mm + Dec 5.934mm) | **Q4 2025 only, computed:** weighted-avg $88.90/share (Oct $95.39, Nov $87.94, Dec $84.18) [computed: Σ(shares×price)/Σshares]; **full-year FY2025 avg price not determinable from this pool** — Q1–Q3 2025 monthly share/price detail is not in the data pool (no earlier 10-Qs present) | Shares out. **fell** intra-year: peak 2,105.7mm (Sep-24) → 2,089.0mm (Dec-24) → 2,058.1mm (Dec-25), a genuine net reduction of 30.9mm shares (−1.5%) from Dec-2024 to Dec-2025 | **Partially — first year buybacks outran SBC on a full-year net-count basis.** FY2025 SBC expense was $1,826M vs. $6,523M spent on buybacks — buybacks now well exceed the annual SBC dollar cost, and the share count fell for the first time | **Buybacks concentrated near a cyclical high, not a low.** The $88.90 weighted-average Q4 2025 price sits well ABOVE the Dec-31-2025 quarter-end close of $69.99 (i.e., the stock fell ~21% from where Uber was actively buying, within the same quarter) and above the current $68.18 price (2026-08-06) [`valuation/02_multiples-own-history.md`] — a ~23% gap between the disclosed Q4 buyback price and the price 8 months later | Net share count fell for the first time in the review window, but the disclosed Q4 price shows repurchases executed well above where the stock subsequently traded, not at a demonstrable low | [FY25 10-K, Item 5, Issuer Purchases of Equity Securities table, p. (Oct/Nov/Dec 2025 monthly detail); FY25 10-K, p. ($1.5bn Q1 2025 ASR; $19.2bn remaining authorization at Dec-31-2025)]; [Historical Capitalization tab] |
| LTM (Jul-25–Jun-26) / H1 FY2026 | 6,904 (of which Q1 FY26 alone = $3,011M, the single largest quarter in the window; Q2 FY26 = $518M) | USD | Not disclosed at share/price granularity for FY2026 in this pool | Not determinable from this pool — no FY2026 monthly repurchase-price table is disclosed in the Q1/Q2 FY26 10-Qs reviewed | Shares out. fell further: 2,058.1mm (Dec-25) → 2,035.6mm (Mar-26), −1.1% in one quarter | **Yes, on net-count basis** — but funded increasingly by debt, not free cash flow alone: strict net debt rose +$3.96bn (+67%) in the year to Jun-30-2026, driven by a $3,997M long-term debt raise in Q2 FY26 that substantially funded the accelerated buyback [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §2] | **Buybacks now occurring while leverage rises materially** — this is the clearest observable discipline concern in the whole record: repurchasing stock is being funded partly by new borrowing rather than solely by the very large free cash flow the company generates ($9,763M FY2025 FCF alone would have covered the LTM buyback without any new debt) | Net share count is falling, which is real progress versus FY2021–FY2024, but the debt-funded acceleration — just as a ~$15bn debt-funded acquisition (Delivery Hero) is being layered on top — raises a capital-structure sequencing question: buying back stock with borrowed money while about to borrow again for a large acquisition is not obviously the highest-return use of the balance sheet | [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §2, §6; CIQ Financials_Quarterly.xls → Cash Flow] |

**RF-CAP-002 test (buybacks not reducing share count) — mixed result depending on window.** On the **full 5-year window** (Dec-2021 1,954.5mm → Mar-2026 2,035.6mm), shares outstanding are still **net higher by +81.1mm (+4.15%)** despite $7.8bn+ of cumulative buybacks — RF-CAP-002 would trigger on this basis. On the **most recent ~15-month window** (peak Mar-2025 2,091.2mm → Mar-2026 2,035.6mm), the share count has fallen **−55.6mm (−2.66%)**, showing genuine recent progress. Both facts are reported; the flag is not triggered outright because the trend has inflected, but the multi-year record shows buybacks only recently began to outpace new-issue dilution, and the two disclosed average buyback prices in this pool ($88.90 in Q4 2025) sit meaningfully above the price the stock has traded at since (Dec-2025 close $69.99; current price $68.18) — a price-discipline concern on the evidence actually disclosed, not a valuation judgment.

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Payout ratio | 0% — Uber has never paid a dividend, FY2016 through LTM Jun-2026 | [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Cash Flow, "Total Dividends Paid" = 0 every year] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | Not applicable — no dividend to cover | — |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital), FY2022→FY2025 | **~29.0%** (Inference/estimate, not a filed metric — methodology below) | See computation and caveats below |

**Incremental ROIC computation (labelled estimate, not from filings).** NOPAT = EBIT × (1 − tax rate), using EBIT (unadjusted: income from operations + D&A basis consistent with Section 1 of `earnings/01_historical-financials.md`) and a flat 21% US statutory tax-rate assumption — a normalization choice made because Uber's actual GAAP effective tax rate is distorted by large, non-cash releases of a deferred-tax-asset valuation allowance ($5,758M benefit in FY2024, $4,346M benefit in FY2025) that have nothing to do with operating capital efficiency [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §3]. FY2022 EBIT −$1,832M → NOPAT ≈ −$1,447M; FY2025 EBIT $5,565M → NOPAT ≈ $4,396M. ΔNOPAT ≈ $5,843M. Invested capital (Total Capital: debt + common equity + preferred + minority interest) rose from $20,221M (Dec-2022) to $40,385M (Dec-2025), ΔInvested capital ≈ $20,164M [CIQ Financials_Annual.xls → Capital Structure Summary, "Total Capital" row]. Incremental ROIC ≈ $5,843M / $20,164M ≈ **29.0%**.

**Caveat (material, must be read with the number above):** this Δ-invested-capital base is NOT a clean organic-reinvestment figure — it mixes (a) retained-earnings-funded organic growth, (b) the debt raised to fund buybacks (roughly capital-structure-neutral, since buybacks reduce equity by a similar amount as debt increases it), and (c) M&A-driven goodwill/intangible additions (e.g., the $712M Trendyol GO goodwill in FY2025 alone). It is best read as a rough, favorable-direction signal that Uber's overall capital base is earning a high accounting return relative to its cost of capital, not as a precise measure of "returns on newly invested organic capital" — labelled **Inference, not from filings**, and the tax-rate normalization further limits precision. Capex itself is small and shrinking ($336M FY2025 vs. $52.0bn revenue) — the real organic-reinvestment story is operating-leverage-driven margin expansion (EBITDA margin 12.1% FY2025, up from a loss in FY2021), not heavy fixed-asset spend [`earnings/01_historical-financials.md`, §1, §6].

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Capital has created real per-share value in the core operating business — EBIT and free cash flow improved every year for four straight years (FY2021–FY2025: EBIT margin −22.0% → +10.7%; FCF −$743M → $9,763M) [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, §4A] — but the *deployment* of the cash that growth has produced is mixed to concerning. The best use of capital has been organic reinvestment: capex is small and shrinking relative to revenue, and the estimated ~29% incremental ROIC (caveated above) shows the operating business earns a high return on the capital already inside it. The worst use has been M&A: a near-continuous acquisition program since 2019 has produced at least one full write-off (Drizly, $1.1bn, shut down within 2.5 years) and one reversed divestiture (Careem, sold down to a minority stake in 2023 and bought back to a controlling stake by mid/late-2026 at a fraction of the original valuation), and it now culminates in a $14.8bn debt-funded bid for Delivery Hero that would roughly double gross debt — a serial-acquirer pattern that trips CLAUDE.md §24 Filter 4 and caps this module's capital-allocation score. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the sequencing risk sitting underneath the recently-improved buyback record: Uber only started genuinely shrinking its share count in the last ~15 months, financed that acceleration partly with new debt (net debt +67% in the year to Jun-2026) at prices ($88.90 disclosed Q4-2025 average) well above where the stock has traded since ($68.18 currently), and is about to layer a ~$15bn acquisition-driven debt bridge on top of that same balance sheet. **Verdict: mixed** — value-creative at the operating level, value-destructive to neutral at the M&A level, and buyback discipline that has only recently improved and remains untested against the coming debt load.

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — UBER

**Partial-data notice.** No compensation disclosure (proxy / DEF 14A) is present in this data pool. Uber's FY2025 10-K explicitly incorporates every governance/comp item by reference to a document not in the pool: Item 11 (Executive Compensation), Item 12 (Security Ownership / Equity Compensation Plan Information), Item 13 (Related-Person Transactions), and Item 14 (Accounting Fees) are all stated as "included... in the 2026 Proxy Statement and... incorporated herein by reference" [FY25 10-K, Items 11–14, p. ITEM 11/12/13/14 section]. That Proxy Statement (a DEF 14A/DEFR14A was filed with the SEC in April 2026, per a web search) was not ingested into this pool and is therefore not available as a citable source under this module's evidence rules. Per this agent's partial-data rule, **incentive alignment cannot be assessed** from the pool. What follows extracts the only high-level structure the 10-K itself discloses (equity-plan mechanics and company-wide stock-based compensation expense — not NEO-specific pay) and uses the web strictly for peer-comparison context, labeled as such. No metric weights are fabricated or asserted for Uber.

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO — Dara Khosrowshahi | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Item 11 incorporated by reference to the 2026 Proxy Statement (not in pool) [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |
| CFO — Balaji Krishnamurthy (in role since Feb 16, 2026) | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Item 11 incorporated by reference to the 2026 Proxy Statement (not in pool) [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |

Reporting currency would be USD; reporting period would be FY2025 (calendar year ended Dec 31, 2025) under a 2026 proxy — both stated for completeness, not as evidence of actual disclosed figures, since no Summary Compensation Table is present in this pool.

**What the 10-K does disclose (equity-plan mechanics, not individual pay):** Uber grants awards under the 2019 Equity Incentive Plan and the 2019 Employee Stock Purchase Plan (ESPP), covering "officers and other employees, directors, and consultants" — not broken out by named executive [FY25 10-K, Note 10 – Stockholders' Equity]. Three award types exist: (1) **service-based** RSUs/options vesting over a multi-year requisite service period; (2) **performance-based** awards (RSAs, RSUs, SARs, options) that vest on both service and "specified performance targets, such as our financial or operating metrics" — the specific metrics are not named in the 10-K; and (3) **market-based** awards that vest on "achievement of specified fully-diluted equity values, as determined based on our stock price," valued using a Monte Carlo model [FY25 10-K, Note 10, Stock-Based Compensation Expense section]. The 10-K states market-based awards granted in FY2023–FY2025 were "not material," implying the legacy market-based structure (economically a form of total-shareholder-return/stock-price hurdle) is a smaller, possibly legacy, component of the current program rather than its core [FY25 10-K, Note 10]. Total fair value of RSUs vested was $1.7 billion in each of FY2023, FY2024, and FY2025 (company-wide, all employees) [FY25 10-K, Note 10]. Total stock-based compensation expense by function: FY2023 $1,935mm, FY2024 $1,796mm, FY2025 $1,826mm (company-wide, all cost centers combined — not CEO/CFO-specific) [FY25 10-K, Note 10, Stock-Based Compensation Expense table].

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus | Not disclosed in pool | Not disclosed in pool | Not assessable | Incorporated by reference to 2026 Proxy Statement, not in pool [FY25 10-K, Item 11] |
| Long-term incentive — performance-based tranche | "financial or operating metrics" (unnamed) | Not disclosed | Not classifiable — the 10-K names the category (performance awards) but not the metric or weight | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |
| Long-term incentive — market-based tranche | Fully-diluted equity value / stock-price hurdle (Monte Carlo-valued) | Not disclosed; described as "not material" for grants in FY2023–FY2025 | This category, where it exists, is stock-price-based — closer to a per-share/returns signal than a size metric, but it is a small and possibly shrinking part of the program on the company's own "not material" characterization | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |
| Long-term incentive — service-based tranche | Time vesting only (no performance condition) | Not disclosed | Not performance-linked at all — pure retention/dilution mechanism, not evidence of either per-share or size alignment | [FY25 10-K, Note 10] |

No bonus-plan metric or LTIP performance-metric weight can be extracted from this pool. The one performance-linked category the 10-K names in outline (performance-based awards tied to "financial or operating metrics") is not decomposable into ROIC/EPS/FCF-per-share versus revenue/EBITDA/deal-count without the Compensation Discussion & Analysis section of the 2026 Proxy Statement, which is absent from the pool.

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| CEO total pay (latest) | Not disclosed in this pool. (A third-party aggregator site references CEO pay data pulled from SEC filings, but that figure is not independently verified against the primary proxy in this pool and is not used here per this agent's rule against citing a vendor number under a filing's name.) | Not assessable from pool [FY25 10-K, Item 11 — incorporated by reference, absent] |
| Pay vs peers (web-sourced context only) | Peer rideshare/delivery platform Lyft disclosed, in its own FY2025 proxy commentary, that performance stock units (PSUs) remain roughly 50% of each named executive's target annual equity value, with an increasing emphasis on performance-linked equity — offered here only as sector-context on what "typical" gig-platform LTIP design currently looks like, not as a statement about Uber's own program. | Web: Lyft DEFC14A / DEF 14A proxy coverage, 2025 (unverified, dated) — peer-benchmarking context only, per this agent's partial-data rule |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | Not assessable — no bonus/LTIP metric, weight, or target is available in the pool to test against realized performance (e.g., the four straight quarters of Adjusted EBITDA guidance beats documented in `01_management-and-track-record.md`). | Not assessable |
| Say-on-pay support (%) | Not disclosed in this pool — say-on-pay vote results are reported in the proxy/AGM voting-results filing, which is not present. | Not assessable [FY25 10-K, Item 11 — incorporated by reference, absent] |

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

Incentive alignment cannot be assessed from the data available in this pool: the 10-K's Items 11–14 all point to the 2026 Proxy Statement for the actual pay tables, bonus/LTIP metrics, weights, targets, ownership, and say-on-pay results, and that document was not ingested. The only structural facts the 10-K itself discloses are that Uber's long-term equity program mixes pure time-vesting awards with performance-based awards tied to unnamed "financial or operating metrics" and a smaller, currently "not material" stock-price-hurdle (market-based) tranche — which is not enough to classify the program as per-share/returns-based or size-based with any confidence. The single biggest incentive signal available is the absence of the primary evidence itself: without the Compensation Discussion & Analysis, this agent cannot say whether Khosrowshahi and Krishnamurthy are paid on revenue/deal growth (empire-building) or on ROIC/EPS/FCF-per-share/TSR (per-share value) — that is the single highest-value follow-up data request for this module. **Conclusion: Insufficient data — not assessable** (neither "well-aligned," "mixed," nor "misaligned" can be supported by evidence in this pool; do not read the absence of red flags here as a clean bill of health).

## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md
